    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Shape stationarity: mechanistic hypothesis.}\nDistributional shape features (entropy standard deviation, tail decay) remain near-stationary across generation depths, while central-tendency features drift monotonically (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}).\nWe hypothesize that this reflects a decomposition of the autoregressive generation process: at each token position, the output distribution is determined jointly by model parameters (fixed) and conditioning context (varying).\nRecursive generation progressively shifts context toward the model's high-probability regions---reducing entropy mean---but does not alter the softmax architecture or sampling hyperparameters that determine distributional shape.\nIn effect, diverse inputs passed through the same softmax and top-$p$ sampler produce NLL distributions whose shape reflects sampler parameters rather than input content, decoupling shape from the content drift that drives central-tendency features.\nThis predicts that shape features should remain stationary until generation depth reaches the model's fixed-point distribution, a regime not yet observed in our chain lengths ($\\leq$4 steps).\nSampling ablation is consistent with this account: depth signal is robust to moderate temperature variation (${\\leq}0.3$\\,pp for $t \\in [0.5, 0.7]$) but degrades substantially at $t = 1.0$ ($-6$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}), where the sampling mechanism itself becomes the dominant source of distributional shape variation, overwhelming the depth signal.\n\n\\paragraph{Comparison with detector ensembles.}\nIn binary evaluation (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+), our 7-feature ensemble (F1\\,=\\,0.723) performs comparably to a 4-detector+surface ensemble (0.724) combining four pretrained detection scores with two surface statistics.\nWe do not claim feature-engineering superiority: the primary contributions are the task formulation (ordinal depth estimation beyond binary detection), the empirical characterization of differential depth response, and interpretability---seven summary statistics from a single lightweight reference model versus four independent detection pipelines.\nThat both ensembles converge on near-identical binary performance suggests depth signal is accessible through multiple feature families; our approach offers a simpler, more interpretable route to the same discriminative boundary.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model choice.}\nQwen3-0.6B serves dual roles as both generator and cross-family reference model in Table~\\ref{tab:cross-ref}, creating a potential self-referencing bias.\nThe measured advantage is modest: Qwen3-0.6B reference achieves LOGO F1\\,=\\,0.816 versus 0.803 for Pythia-410M (+1.3\\,pp), within the range of inter-reference variation (spread 0.013).\nAll main results use Pythia-410M as the default reference to avoid this overlap; the cross-reference experiment (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}) serves solely to confirm that differential depth response is reference-model-independent.\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt-type dependence.}\nDifferential depth response strength varies substantially across prompt types.\nContinuation chains exhibit 16.9\\% depth retention loss by gen-4, while elaboration chains retain 98.6\\% of samples (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-type}).\nThe 7-feature ensemble achieves within-type CV F1 of 0.781 for continuation vs.\\ 0.730 for elaboration; cross-prompt-type transfer (LOPTO F1\\,=\\,0.753) confirms shared signal despite differing drift magnitudes.\nTogether with the paraphrastic attenuation reported below (binary F1\\,=\\,0.671 vs.\\ 0.749 for continuation), these results bound depth fingerprinting's primary applicability to continuation-style generation, where distributional drift is strongest; near-paraphrastic settings approach the method's detection floor as semantic-preservation constraints anchor distributional shift \\cite{broken_telephone}.\n\n\\paragraph{Corpus-level vs.\\ document-level utility.}\nThe practical value of depth estimation depends on the unit of analysis.\nAt the individual document level, gen-1 per-class F1\\,=\\,0.64 implies a 36\\% misclassification rate---insufficient for automated filtering but suitable for flagging suspect documents for human review.\nAt the corpus level, aggregate depth composition can be estimated with substantially lower error: over $n$ documents, estimation error scales as $O(1/\\sqrt{n})$---analogous to opinion polling, where individual voter prediction is unreliable but population-level estimates are actionable---making corpus-level provenance characterization reliable even with moderate per-document accuracy.\nThe 3-class design explicitly targets the most informative boundaries: human vs.\\ machine (gen-0 vs.\\ gen-1+) and shallow vs.\\ deep recursion (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+), where the distributional distinction is most actionable.\n\n\\paragraph{Relation to model collapse.}\nOur results relate to, but are distinct from, model collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse,self_consuming_mad}.\nIn training-loop collapse, gradient updates on self-generated data cause progressive mode collapse---catastrophic diversity loss after 5--10 retraining generations \\cite{curse_of_recursion}.\nOur inference-chain setting involves no parameter updates; drift instead accumulates through repeated sampling from the model's high-probability regions, yielding a milder effect (hapax ratio declines ${\\sim}$14\\% over 4 depths).\nBoth processes share declining diversity, but they diverge in distributional shape: training collapse alters the distribution's functional form as learned parameters shift, whereas inference chains preserve shape stationarity because the decoding mechanism remains fixed.\nThis distinction suggests inference-chain drift features could serve as leading indicators: text exhibiting these signatures would, if recycled into training data, seed the more severe collapse regime.\nTopic drift mediates rather than confounds this process: semantic narrowing accounts for less than 9\\% of feature variance (maximum $R^2 = 0.082$ for entropy\\_mean), and shape features are near-independent of semantic drift ($|\\rho| = 0.068$).\n\n\\paragraph{Granularity ceiling.}\nFive-class depth estimation reduces LOGO F1 from 0.789 to 0.501; gen-2 and gen-3 are inseparable (per-class F1\\,$\\approx$\\,0.26), establishing three classes as the practical ceiling for handcrafted features (Appendix~\\ref{app:finer-depth}).\nQLoRA fine-tuning raises 3-class F1 to 0.905 (+11.6\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}), confirming exploitable depth signal beyond summary statistics.\n\n\\paragraph{Robustness to post-generation editing.}\nDepth features degrade gracefully under simulated editing: 20\\% random token replacement reduces 3-class F1 by only 6.0\\,pp, while LLM rephrasing drops binary F1 by 6.3\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:human-edit}).\nThis asymmetry---surface noise is tolerable, systematic distributional manipulation is not---is consistent with depth signal residing in aggregate distributional properties rather than individual token choices.\n\n\\paragraph{Paraphrastic chains.}\nInitial paraphrastic experiments exhibited reversed entropy--depth correlations ($\\rho = +0.394$), but removing contaminated samples aligned correlations with continuation chains ($\\rho = -0.533$), confirming the reversal was an artifact.\nOn clean data, paraphrastic chains achieve binary F1\\,=\\,0.671 (vs.\\ 0.749 for continuation), consistent with faster convergence under semantic-preservation constraints \\cite{broken_telephone}.\nCross-transfer is effective (para$\\to$cont F1\\,=\\,0.770, cont$\\to$para 0.743), indicating shared depth signal.\n\n\\paragraph{Instruct tuning.}\nInstruction-tuned models show substantially weaker depth fingerprints (OLMo-Instruct LOGO F1\\,=\\,0.338 vs.\\ 0.764 base; Qwen3-0.6B-Instruct 0.667 vs.\\ 0.816 base under the 7-generator split; base-only yields 0.824, Table~\\ref{tab:logo-results}).\nLength-matched controls reduce correlations to near zero (all $|\\rho| < 0.02$ for Qwen3-0.6B), and binary classification is near chance (F1\\,$\\approx$\\,0.5), indicating that depth signal is not operationally detectable in instruct chains.\nThis justifies our restriction to base models: instruction tuning introduces distributional effects (output length regulation, format constraints) that dominate the subtler recursive drift signal, making base models the appropriate setting for isolating the generation mechanism.\n\n\\paragraph{Negative downstream finding.}\nDepth-aware curation does not improve downstream language model quality over random subsampling (\\S\\ref{sec:results-downstream}).\nThis negative result carries positive information value: it rules out depth-based filtering as a data selection strategy, sparing practitioners from building depth-conditional pipelines that would not yield quality gains.\nThe dominant factor is data quantity, not depth composition---consistent with findings emphasizing data volume over curation sophistication \\cite{self_consuming_mad}.\n",

          " Sampling ablation is consistent with this account: depth signal is robust to moderate temperature variation (${\\leq}0.3$\\,pp for $t \\in [0.5, 0.7]$) but degrades substantially at $t = 1.0$ ($-6$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}), where the sampling mechanism itself becomes the dominant source of distributional shape variation, overwhelming the depth signal.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Comparison with detector ensembles.}"
        ]
      }
    ],

Per-generator variation for entropy\\_std is attenuated when pooled across generators (within-generator $|\\rho|$ up to 0.30 vs.\\ pooled $|\\rho| = 0.076$).}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe evaluate depth fingerprinting along four axes: cross-generator classification (\\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}), differential depth response of individual features (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}), comparison with existing detection baselines (\\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}), and downstream utility for data curation (\\S\\ref{sec:results-downstream}).\n\n\\subsection{Depth Classification Performance}\n\\label{sec:results-classification}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:logo-results} reports leave-one-generator-out (LOGO) macro-F1 for 3-class depth estimation (gen-0\\,/\\,gen-1\\,/\\,gen-2+) across six generators spanning three model families and parameter counts from 0.6B to 8.2B.\n\nThe 7-feature ensemble achieves 3-class LOGO F1\\,=\\,0.789 (Table~\\ref{tab:logo-results}). Because gen-0 seed texts are shared across generators (\\S\\ref{sec:generation}), the gen-0 class F1 (0.94) may be inflated; we therefore report a leak-free binary evaluation (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+, F1\\,=\\,0.723) as the primary metric in \\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}.\nPer-generator binary F1 ranges from 0.641 (Qwen3-8B) to 0.795 (Pythia-1B); 3-class F1 ranges from 0.683 to 0.856.\nPer-class F1 varies by depth: gen-0\\,=\\,0.94, gen-2+\\,=\\,0.80, gen-1\\,=\\,0.64; the primary difficulty lies in distinguishing gen-1 from gen-2+, where feature margins narrow.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-scale transfer.}\nThe six-generator evaluation includes Qwen3-8B (8.2B parameters), confirming that depth signal persists beyond the $\\leq$2.8B training generators, though with a moderate gap: Qwen3-8B binary F1\\,=\\,0.641 (CI [0.625, 0.657]) vs.\\ 0.68--0.80 for smaller generators.\nAdding Qwen3-8B data improves classification on the original five generators (+0.8\\,pp to 0.810); a volume-matched control confirms 94\\% of this gain is attributable to generator diversity rather than data quantity.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-family generalization.}\nLeave-one-family-out (LOFO) evaluation---holding out an entire model family and training on the remaining two---yields macro-F1 of 0.772 (Pythia held out), 0.762 (OLMo held out), and 0.754 (Qwen held out), confirming that depth features transfer across architectural families with only modest degradation (average $\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$2.6\\,pp vs.\\ LOGO).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model chains.}\nCross-model chains---where each generation step uses a randomly selected generator---yield 3-class F1\\,=\\,0.809 and binary F1\\,=\\,0.764 under 5-fold stratified cross-validation, confirming that depth signal persists under generator heterogeneity.\n\n\\paragraph{Length confound control.}\nText length correlates weakly with depth (Spearman $|\\rho| < 0.14$).\nLength-normalized feature trends are preserved, and length-matched subsampling yields comparable or higher F1 ($\\Delta$\\,=\\,+0.041), ruling out length as a confound (Appendix~\\ref{app:length}).\n\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Generator} & \\textbf{Family} & \\textbf{Params} & \\textbf{3-class F1} & \\textbf{Binary F1} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1B       & Pythia & 1.0B & 0.856 & \\textbf{0.795} & [0.780, 0.809] \\\\\nPythia-2.8B     & Pythia & 2.8B & 0.804 & \\textbf{0.723} & [0.709, 0.739] \\\\\nOLMo-2-1B      & OLMo   & 1.5B & 0.764 & \\textbf{0.677} & [0.660, 0.692] \\\\\nQwen3-0.6B     & Qwen   & 0.6B & 0.824 & \\textbf{0.756} & [0.742, 0.772] \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B     & Qwen   & 1.7B & 0.806 & \\textbf{0.748} & [0.732, 0.762] \\\\\nQwen3-8B       & Qwen   & 8.2B & 0.683 & \\textbf{0.641} & [0.625, 0.657] \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{Average (6-gen LOGO)} & 0.789 & \\textbf{0.723} & --- \\\\\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{5-gen LOGO (excl.\\ Qwen3-8B)} & 0.802 & \\textbf{0.740} & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{6-generator LOGO macro-F1 for the 7-feature ensemble (bootstrap 95\\% CIs, $n$\\,=\\,1000). Gen-0 samples are shared across generators (\\S\\ref{sec:generation}); 3-class results are supplementary. Binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+) is the primary metric. (6-generator LOGO; 3-class and binary.)}\n\\label{tab:logo-results}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Ordinal regression comparison.}\nCORAL ordinal regression \\cite{coral_ordinal} and Frank-Hall ordinal reduction \\cite{frank_hall_ordinal} achieve 3-class F1 of 0.771 and 0.793 respectively, with all CIs overlapping the standard XGBoost (0.789).\nExplicitly encoding ordinal structure does not yield a measurable gain, likely because 3-class granularity provides insufficient ordinal resolution for ranking-aware losses.\n\n\n\\subsection{Differential Depth Response}\n\\label{sec:results-differential}\n\nFeatures exhibit \\emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity measures drift monotonically with depth, while distributional shape measures remain near-stationary.\nThis pattern is consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift in the token-level NLL distribution---the text becomes progressively more predictable (mean shifts), while the functional form of the distribution is preserved (shape is invariant).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Differential depth response: Spearman correlations between individual features and generation depth. Central-tendency and diversity features (hapax ratio, TTR, entropy mean) show strong monotonic drift, while shape features (entropy std, tail decay) remain near-stationary.}\n\\label{fig:feature-drift}\n\\end{figure}\n\nIndividual-level Spearman correlations with depth confirm the split (Figure~\\ref{fig:feature-drift}):\ndrifting features show strong monotonic trends (hapax ratio $\\rho = -0.558$, TTR $\\rho = -0.542$, entropy mean $\\rho = -0.475$),\nwhile shape features show weak or negligible correlations (entropy std $|\\rho| = 0.076$, tail decay $|\\rho| = 0.089$).\nHowever, these correlations are computed on continuation chains; elaboration and rewriting chains show substantially weaker drift (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-type}), and this prompt-type dependence bounds the generality of the differential response characterization.\n\n\\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} reports ablation results.\nThe full 7-feature set (F1\\,=\\,0.789) significantly outperforms both surface-only features (TTR, hapax\\_ratio; F1\\,=\\,0.729) and reference-only features (entropy mean/std/max/min, tail\\_decay; F1\\,=\\,0.745; McNemar $p \\approx 0$).\nPermutation importance ranks hapax ratio highest (0.269), followed by entropy mean (0.164) and TTR (0.159).\nSurface features capture vocabulary impoverishment; reference features capture distributional shift---neither subsumes the other.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}\nTo confirm that differential depth response is not an artifact of the Pythia-410M reference model, we recompute all features using two cross-family references: OLMo-2-1B (1.5B) and Qwen3-0.6B.\nAll three reference models achieve comparable LOGO F1: Pythia-410M\\,=\\,0.803, OLMo-2-1B\\,=\\,0.807, Qwen3-0.6B\\,=\\,0.816 (spread 0.013; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-ref}).\nEntropy drift direction is consistently negative across all reference models (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-ref}), confirming that the differential depth response is a property of recursive generation, not of the specific reference model used.\n\n\\input{table4}\n\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Feature set} & \\textbf{LOGO F1} & \\textbf{Top feature (importance)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFull (7 features)     & 0.789 & hapax\\_ratio (0.269) \\\\\nSurface only (2)      & 0.729 & hapax\\_ratio (0.320) \\\\\nReference only (5)    & 0.745 & entropy\\_mean (0.218) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Feature ablation. Full $>$ surface = reference confirms complementarity between surface diversity features and reference-model distributional features. (6-generator LOGO; 3-class.)}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\nLeave-one-feature-out analysis confirms that 6 of 7 features contribute positively, with entropy\\_max showing the largest marginal impact despite modest split frequency (Appendix~\\ref{app:lofo}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}\n\\label{sec:results-baselines}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_baseline_comparison.png}\n\\caption{LOGO macro-F1 with 95\\% bootstrap confidence intervals for each detection baseline and the 7-feature ensemble. Per-generator held-out F1 varies more for single detectors than for the ensemble.}\n\\label{fig:baseline-ci}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe repurpose four existing detection scores as ordinal depth predictors by training the same classifier on each score as a single feature, using identical LOGO evaluation.\nBecause gen-0 seed texts are shared across generators (\\S\\ref{sec:generation}), 3-class LOGO evaluation creates a train--test overlap for the gen-0 class.\nWe therefore report both 3-class and binary (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+) results; the binary metric is the primary measure of depth discrimination (Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{3-class F1} & \\textbf{Binary F1} & \\textbf{Per-gen std} \\\\\n\\midrule\n7-feature ensemble    & 0.789 & \\textbf{0.723} & 0.051 \\\\\n4-det.\\ + surface     & \\textbf{0.805} & 0.724 & 0.049 \\\\\n4-detector ens.\\      & 0.796 & 0.712 & 0.044 \\\\\nAvg-LogProb           & 0.737 & 0.712 & 0.044 \\\\\nLD-Score              & 0.655 & 0.701 & 0.045 \\\\\nFastDetectGPT         & 0.688 & 0.616 & 0.056 \\\\\nBinoculars            & 0.417 & 0.524 & 0.022 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Depth classification under unified LOGO evaluation. \\textbf{3-class}: gen-0/gen-1/gen-2+ (gen-0 samples shared across generators; see \\S\\ref{sec:generation}). \\textbf{Binary}: gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+ only, eliminating the gen-0 train--test overlap. Per-gen std measures cross-generator consistency of the binary metric. The 7-feature ensemble matches the multi-detector ensemble on binary F1 (0.723 vs.\\ 0.724); the contribution is simplicity (7 summary statistics from one reference model) rather than performance superiority. (6-generator LOGO; 3-class and binary.)}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe 7-feature ensemble achieves binary F1\\,=\\,0.723, outperforming every individual baseline ($p < 0.001$ for all; Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}).\nCombining all four detection scores into a 4-detector ensemble yields binary F1\\,=\\,0.712---below the 7-feature ensemble; adding surface features (TTR, hapax ratio) raises it to 0.724, matching the 7-feature ensemble and confirming that surface diversity features capture signal complementary to detection scores.\nThe multi-detector 3-class advantage (0.796 vs.\\ 0.789) is driven entirely by gen-0 classification; existing detectors do not capture the finer depth signal between gen-1 and gen-2+.\n\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Utility}\n\\label{sec:results-downstream}\n\nDepth-aware data curation does not improve downstream language model quality.\nFine-tuning OLMo-2-0425-1B on five data variants (3 seeds each) shows that data quantity dominates depth composition: random subsampling (ppl\\,=\\,2.367) outperforms depth-stratified selection (ppl\\,=\\,2.387) at equal sample count (Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream}).\n",

[0.709, 0.739] \\\\\nOLMo-2-1B      & OLMo   & 1.5B & 0.764 & \\textbf{0.677} & [0.660, 0.692] \\\\\nQwen3-0.6B     & Qwen   & 0.6B & 0.824 & \\textbf{0.756} & [0.742, 0.772] \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B     & Qwen   & 1.7B & 0.806 & \\textbf{0.748} & [0.732, 0.762] \\\\\nQwen3-8B       & Qwen   & 8.2B & 0.683 & \\textbf{0.641} & [0.625, 0.657] \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{Average (6-gen LOGO)} & 0.789 & \\textbf{0.723} & --- \\\\\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{5-gen LOGO (excl.\\ Qwen3-8B)} & 0.802 & \\textbf{0.740} & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{6-generator LOGO macro-F1 for the 7-feature ensemble (bootstrap 95\\% CIs, $n$\\,=\\,1000). Gen-0 samples are shared across generators (\\S\\ref{sec:generation}); 3-class results are supplementary. Binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+) is the primary metric. (6-generator LOGO; 3-class and binary.)}\n\\label{tab:logo-results}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Ordinal regression comparison.}\nCORAL ordinal regression \\cite{coral_ordinal} and Frank-Hall ordinal reduction \\cite{frank_hall_ordinal} achieve 3-class F1 of 0.771 and 0.793 respectively, with all CIs overlapping the standard XGBoost (0.789).\nExplicitly encoding ordinal structure does not yield a measurable gain, likely because 3-class granularity provides insufficient ordinal resolution for ranking-aware losses.\n\n\n\\subsection{Differential Depth Response}\n\\label{sec:results-differential}\n\nFeatures exhibit \\emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity measures drift monotonically with depth, while distributional shape measures remain near-stationary.\nThis pattern is consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift in the token-level NLL distribution---the text becomes progressively more predictable (mean shifts), while the functional form of the distribution is preserved (shape is invariant).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Differential depth response: Spearman correlations between individual features and generation depth. Drifting features (hapax ratio $\\rho = -0.558$, TTR $-0.542$, entropy mean $-0.475$, entropy min $-0.303$) show monotonic trends with depth, while near-stationary features (entropy max $|\\rho| = 0.095$, tail decay $0.089$, entropy std $0.076$) show weak or negligible correlations. Per-generator variation for entropy\\_std is attenuated when pooled across generators (within-generator $|\\rho|$ up to 0.30 vs.\\ pooled $|\\rho| = 0.076$).}\n\\label{fig:feature-drift}\n\\end{figure}\n\nIndividual-level Spearman correlations with depth confirm the split (Figure~\\ref{fig:feature-drift}):\ndrifting features show strong monotonic trends (hapax ratio $\\rho = -0.558$, TTR $\\rho = -0.542$, entropy mean $\\rho = -0.475$),\nwhile shape features show weak or negligible correlations (entropy std $|\\rho| = 0.076$, tail decay $|\\rho| = 0.089$).\nHowever, these correlations are computed on continuation chains; elaboration and rewriting chains show substantially weaker drift (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-type}), and this prompt-type dependence bounds the generality of the differential response characterization.\n\n\\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} reports ablation results.\nThe full 7-feature set (F1\\,=\\,0.789) significantly outperforms both surface-only features (TTR, hapax\\_ratio; F1\\,=\\,0.729) and reference-only features (entropy mean/std/max/min, tail\\_decay; F1\\,=\\,0.745; McNemar $p \\approx 0$).\nPermutation importance ranks hapax ratio highest (0.269), followed by entropy mean (0.164) and TTR (0.159).\nSurface features capture vocabulary impoverishment; reference features capture distributional shift---neither subsumes the other.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}\nTo confirm that differential depth response is not an artifact of the Pythia-410M reference model, we recompute all features using two cross-family references: OLMo-2-1B (1.5B) and Qwen3-0.6B.\nAll three reference models achieve comparable LOGO F1: Pythia-410M\\,=\\,0.803, OLMo-2-1B\\,=\\,0.807, Qwen3-0.6B\\,=\\,0.816 (spread 0.013; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-ref}).\nEntropy drift direction is consistently negative across all reference models (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-ref}), confirming that the differential depth response is a property of recursive generation, not of the specific reference model used.\n\n\\input{table4}\n\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Feature set} & \\textbf{LOGO F1} & \\textbf{Top feature (importance)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFull (7 features)     & 0.789 & hapax\\_ratio (0.269) \\\\\nSurface only (2)      & 0.729 & hapax\\_ratio (0.320) \\\\\nReference only (5)    & 0.745 & entropy\\_mean (0.218) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Feature ablation. Full $>$ surface = reference confirms complementarity between surface diversity features and reference-model distributional features. (6-generator LOGO; 3-class.)}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\nLeave-one-feature-out analysis confirms that 6 of 7 features contribute positively, with entropy\\_max showing the largest marginal impact despite modest split frequency (Appendix~\\ref{app:lofo}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}\n\\label{sec:results-baselines}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_baseline_comparison.png}\n\\caption{LOGO macro-F1 with 95\\% bootstrap confidence intervals for each detection baseline and the 7-feature ensemble. Per-generator held-out F1 varies more for single detectors than for the ensemble.}\n\\label{fig:baseline-ci}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe repurpose four existing detection scores as ordinal depth predictors by training the same classifier on each score as a single feature, using identical LOGO evaluation.\nBecause gen-0 seed texts are shared across generators (\\S\\ref{sec:generation}), 3-class LOGO evaluation creates a train--test overlap for the gen-0 class.\nWe therefore report both 3-class and binary (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+) results; the binary metric is the primary measure of depth discrimination (Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{3-class F1} & \\textbf{Binary F1} & \\textbf{Per-gen std} \\\\\n\\midrule\n7-feature ensemble    & 0.789 & \\textbf{0.723} & 0.051 \\\\\n4-det.\\ + surface     & \\textbf{0.805} & 0.724 & 0.049 \\\\\n4-detector ens.\\      & 0.796 & 0.712 & 0.044 \\\\\nAvg-LogProb           & 0.737 & 0.712 & 0.044 \\\\\nLD-Score              & 0.655 & 0.701 & 0.045 \\\\\nFastDetectGPT         & 0.688 & 0.616 & 0.056 \\\\\nBinoculars            & 0.417 & 0.524 & 0.022 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Depth classification under unified LOGO evaluation. \\textbf{3-class}: gen-0/gen-1/gen-2+ (gen-0 samples shared across generators; see \\S\\ref{sec:generation}). \\textbf{Binary}: gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+ only, eliminating the gen-0 train--test overlap. Per-gen std measures cross-generator consistency of the binary metric. The 7-feature ensemble matches the multi-detector ensemble on binary F1 (0.723 vs.\\ 0.724); the contribution is simplicity (7 summary statistics from one reference model) rather than performance superiority. (6-generator LOGO; 3-class and binary.)}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe 7-feature ensemble achieves binary F1\\,=\\,0.723, outperforming every individual baseline ($p < 0.001$ for all; Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}).\nCombining all four detection scores into a 4-detector ensemble yields binary F1\\,=\\,0.712---below the 7-feature ensemble; adding surface features (TTR, hapax ratio) raises it to 0.724, matching the 7-feature ensemble and confirming that surface diversity features capture signal complementary to detection scores.\nThe multi-detector 3-class advantage (0.796 vs.\\ 0.789) is driven entirely by gen-0 classification; existing detectors do not capture the finer depth signal between gen-1 and gen-2+.\n\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Utility}\n\\label{sec:results-downstream}\n\nDepth-aware data curation does not improve downstream language model quality.\nFine-tuning OLMo-2-0425-1B on five data variants (3 seeds each) shows that data quantity dominates depth composition: random subsampling (ppl\\,=\\,2.367) outperforms depth-stratified selection (ppl\\,=\\,2.387) at equal sample count (Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream}).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Task Formulation}\n\\label{sec:task}\n\nGiven a text $x$, the goal is to predict its \\emph{generation depth} $d \\in \\{0, 1, 2{+}\\}$, where $d{=}0$ denotes human-written text, $d{=}1$ denotes first-generation LLM output conditioned on human text, and $d{=}2{+}$ denotes text produced by recursively conditioning an LLM on its own prior output at least twice.\nFormally, let $\\mathcal{C}_0$ be a reference corpus of human-written text.\nA generation chain of depth $k$ is defined recursively: $x^{(0)} \\sim \\mathcal{C}_0$, and $x^{(k)} = G(x^{(k-1)})$ for $k \\geq 1$, where $G$ denotes stochastic LLM generation conditioned on its input.\nWe frame depth estimation as an ordinal classification task over three classes rather than binary real-vs-synthetic detection, enabling finer-grained provenance characterization.\nDepths beyond 2 are merged into a single $2{+}$ class, consistent with monotonic feature convergence that compresses inter-class margins beyond depth 2 (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}).\n\n\\subsection{Generation Chain Construction}\n\\label{sec:generation}\n\nWe construct generation chains using six generators spanning three model families: \\textbf{Pythia} (2.8B, 1B) \\cite{pythia}, \\textbf{OLMo} (OLMo-2-1B) \\cite{olmo}, and \\textbf{Qwen} (Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B-Base) \\cite{qwen3}.\nThis selection covers parameter scales from 0.6B to 8.2B and diverse pretraining corpora, enabling evaluation of cross-generator and cross-scale generalization.\n\nFor each generator, we sample approximately 1{,}000 seed passages from English Wikipedia as $\\mathcal{C}_0$.\nThe same pool of seed passages serves as gen-0 for all generators.\nIn LOGO evaluation, gen-0 samples from the held-out generator's chains also appear (with the same text) in the training generators' chains, creating a potential train--test overlap for the gen-0 class.\nWe address this with a supplementary binary evaluation excluding gen-0 entirely (\\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}).\nEach seed is iteratively fed through the generator to produce a chain of depth 4 (i.e., gen-0 through gen-4), using the prompt ``\\texttt{Continue the following text: \\{text\\}}''.\nAll generation uses temperature $\\tau{=}0.7$, nucleus sampling with $p{=}0.9$, and maximum length of 256 new tokens.\nThe theoretical yield is $6 \\times {\\sim}1{,}000 \\times 5 \\approx 30{,}000$ texts; after removing generation failures, exact duplicates, and passages shorter than 50 tokens, 27{,}529 texts remain.\nFor classification, depths 2--4 are collapsed into the $2{+}$ class.\n\n\\subsection{Feature Extraction}\n\\label{sec:features}\n\nThe seven features are motivated by two complementary views of distributional drift.\nReference-model features capture how the text's token-level surprise distribution shifts relative to a fixed model; surface features capture how lexical diversity contracts under recursive generation.\nTogether, they span both the generative mechanism (what the model produces) and the surface manifestation (how the vocabulary changes).\nThis two-pronged design is not post-hoc: it follows directly from the hypothesis that recursive generation induces a location shift in the NLL distribution (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}).\n\nWe extract seven distributional features per text, organized into two groups based on what they measure rather than how they are computed.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference-model features.}\nA held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.\nAlthough Pythia-410M shares the GPT-NeoX architecture with two of the six generators, cross-family reference model experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}) confirm that depth features are not reference-specific (LOGO F1 varies by only 0.013 across Pythia, OLMo, and Qwen references).\nFrom the resulting NLL sequence, we extract five statistics:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{entropy\\_mean}\\footnote{We use ``entropy'' as shorthand for token-level negative log-likelihood under the reference model, following convention in prior detection work. These are point-wise NLL values, not Shannon entropy of the full distribution.}: mean token-level NLL, capturing the average predictability of the text under the reference model;\n    \\item \\textbf{entropy\\_std}: standard deviation of token-level NLL, measuring the spread of per-token surprise;\n    \\item \\textbf{entropy\\_max}: maximum token-level NLL, capturing the most surprising token;\n    \\item \\textbf{entropy\\_min}: minimum token-level NLL, capturing the most predictable token---as generation depth increases, the floor of token-level surprise drops, reflecting the model's increasing confidence on its own outputs.\n    Although entropy\\_min shows near-zero marginal impact in leave-one-feature-out analysis ($\\Delta$F1\\,=\\,+0.01\\,pp), we retain it for completeness of the NLL summary statistics (mean, std, max, min) and because removing it does not improve performance;\n    \\item \\textbf{tail\\_decay}: proportion of tokens whose NLL exceeds the 90th percentile of the reference distribution (computed over $\\mathcal{C}_0$), measuring how heavy-tailed the NLL distribution is relative to human text.\n    Sensitivity analysis confirms stable classification (F1 variation ${<}$0.3\\,pp) across thresholds from the 85th to 95th percentile.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\paragraph{Surface features.}\nTwo features capture lexical diversity without requiring a reference model:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{TTR}: type-token ratio (number of unique word types divided by total tokens);\n    \\item \\textbf{hapax\\_ratio}: proportion of words appearing exactly once (\\emph{hapax legomena}).\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}\nThe grouping reflects an empirical pattern we term \\emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity features (entropy\\_mean, TTR, hapax\\_ratio) drift monotonically with generation depth, while distributional shape features (entropy\\_std, tail\\_decay) remain near-stationary.\nThis differential behavior---consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift in the NLL distribution while preserving its functional form---motivates combining both feature types.\nAs shown in \\S\\ref{sec:results}, the full 7-feature set significantly outperforms either group alone.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}\n\\label{sec:evaluation}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-validation.}\nTo evaluate generalization to unseen generators, we use \\textbf{Leave-One-Generator-Out (LOGO)} cross-validation: in each fold, one of the six generators is held out for testing and the remaining five provide training data.\nWe additionally report \\textbf{Leave-One-Family-Out (LOFO)} results, where an entire model family (e.g., all Qwen models) is held out, testing generalization across architectural lineages.\n\n\\paragraph{Classifier.}\nWe use XGBoost \\cite{xgboost} as the classifier throughout.\nGradient-boosted trees handle heterogeneous feature scales and nonlinear interactions without extensive hyperparameter tuning, and their feature importance scores support interpretability.\nWe use $n\\_\\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\\max\\_\\text{depth}{=}6$, and learning rate $0.1$ for the 7-feature ensemble.\nBaseline detectors (\\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}) use the same XGBoost framework with $n\\_\\text{estimators}{=}100$ and $\\max\\_\\text{depth}{=}3$ to avoid overfitting their single-feature input.\nLOGO F1 is stable across random seeds (mean\\,=\\,0.789, std\\,=\\,0.0009 over 5 seeds), confirming that results are not sensitive to classifier initialization.\nAlternative classifiers (random forest, logistic regression) yield comparable performance (Appendix~\\ref{app:classifier}), confirming that discriminative power resides in the features, not the classifier.\nWe evaluated two ordinal-specific methods (CORAL \\cite{coral_ordinal}, Frank-Hall ordinal ensemble \\cite{frank_hall_ordinal}); neither significantly outperforms multiclass XGBoost (3-class LOGO F1 0.771--0.793 vs.\\ 0.789, CIs overlapping), confirming that with only three ordinal levels, ordinal structure provides minimal additional benefit.\n\n\\paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}\nThe primary metric is \\textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes, which weights minority classes (gen-1, gen-2+) equally against the typically easier gen-0 class.\nWe report per-class F1 scores and 95\\% confidence intervals via \\emph{percentile bootstrap} ($n{=}1{,}000$), resampling at the document level within each held-out fold.\nFor pairwise model comparisons (e.g., full features vs.\\ ablated subsets), we use the \\textbf{McNemar test} on disagreement tables.\nFor comparisons against detection baselines, we use \\textbf{paired bootstrap} significance tests.\nAll reported $p$-values are two-sided.\nWe report six pairwise comparisons in \\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}; applying Bonferroni correction ($\\alpha_{\\text{adj}} = 0.05/6 = 0.0083$), all remain significant at $p < 0.001$.\nFeature--depth correlations (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}) are computed on class-balanced subsamples (equal samples per depth class) to control for depth-class imbalance in the generation pipeline.\n\n\\paragraph{Baselines.}\nWe compare the 7-feature ensemble against four single-score detection methods repurposed as ordinal depth predictors: Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT \\cite{fast_detectgpt}, Binoculars \\cite{binoculars}, and LD-Score \\cite{ld_score}.\nEach baseline score is used as a univariate feature in the same XGBoost framework under LOGO cross-validation, ensuring a controlled comparison.\n\n\\paragraph{Length confound control.}\nSince TTR and hapax\\_ratio are mechanically length-dependent, we verify that depth signal is not an artifact of systematic length variation.\nWe conduct three complementary analyses: (1) Spearman correlation between text length and each feature (requiring $|\\rho| < 0.15$ for non-confounded features), (2) classification with length-normalized features, and (3) comparison on length-matched subsets.\n",

  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Shape stationarity: mechanistic hypothesis.}\nDistributional shape features (entropy standard deviation, tail decay) remain near-stationary across generation depths, while central-tendency features drift monotonically (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}).\nWe hypothesize that this reflects a decomposition of the autoregressive generation process: at each token position, the output distribution is determined jointly by model parameters (fixed) and conditioning context (varying).\nRecursive generation progressively shifts context toward the model's high-probability regions---reducing entropy mean---but does not alter the softmax architecture or sampling hyperparameters that determine distributional shape.\nIn effect, diverse inputs passed through the same softmax and top-$p$ sampler produce NLL distributions whose shape reflects sampler parameters rather than input content, decoupling shape from the content drift that drives central-tendency features.\nThis predicts that shape features should remain stationary until generation depth reaches the model's fixed-point distribution, a regime not yet observed in our chain lengths ($\\leq$4 steps).",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Shape stationarity: mechanistic hypothesis.}\nNear-stationary features (entropy standard deviation, entropy max, tail decay) remain stable across generation depths, while drifting features---including entropy min---shift monotonically (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}).\nThe refined pattern is \\emph{lower-tail compression with upper-tail preservation}: entropy\\_min (the NLL floor) decreases with depth ($\\rho = -0.303$; per-generator $|\\rho|$ up to 0.51), indicating that the model's most confident predictions become progressively more confident under recursion; meanwhile entropy\\_max (the NLL ceiling) is unchanged ($|\\rho| = 0.095$), and entropy\\_std (spread) remains near-stationary at the pooled level ($|\\rho| = 0.076$).\nMechanistically, recursive generation compresses the lower tail of the NLL distribution while preserving upper-tail behavior, resulting in a net location shift with partial shape preservation.\nThe softmax and top-$p$ sampler impose an effective ceiling on per-token surprise, explaining upper-tail invariance; but conditioning on progressively more predictable context allows the model to produce increasingly confident (low-NLL) tokens, compressing the lower tail.\nWe note a Simpson's paradox in entropy\\_std: within individual generators, $|\\rho|$ reaches up to 0.30, but cross-generator pooling reduces this to 0.076. Within-generator shape drift is partially masked by between-generator variability in baseline distributional shape.\nThis predicts that shape features should remain approximately stationary until generation depth reaches the model's fixed-point distribution, a regime not yet observed in our chain lengths ($\\leq$4 steps)."
}

Within-generator shape drift is partially masked by between-generator variability in baseline distributional shape.\nThis predicts that shape features should remain approximately stationary until generation depth reaches the model's fixed-point distribution, a regime not yet observed in our chain lengths ($\\leq$4 steps).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Shape stationarity: mechanistic hypothesis.}\nDistributional shape features (entropy standard deviation, tail decay) remain near-stationary across generation depths, while central-tendency features drift monotonically (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}).\nWe hypothesize that this reflects a decomposition of the autoregressive generation process: at each token position, the output distribution is determined jointly by model parameters (fixed) and conditioning context (varying).\nRecursive generation progressively shifts context toward the model's high-probability regions---reducing entropy mean---but does not alter the softmax architecture or sampling hyperparameters that determine distributional shape.\nIn effect, diverse inputs passed through the same softmax and top-$p$ sampler produce NLL distributions whose shape reflects sampler parameters rather than input content, decoupling shape from the content drift that drives central-tendency features.\nThis predicts that shape features should remain stationary until generation depth reaches the model's fixed-point distribution, a regime not yet observed in our chain lengths ($\\leq$4 steps).\nSampling ablation is consistent with this account: depth signal is robust to moderate temperature variation (${\\leq}0.3$\\,pp for $t \\in [0.5, 0.7]$) but degrades substantially at $t = 1.0$ ($-6$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}), where the sampling mechanism itself becomes the dominant source of distributional shape variation, overwhelming the depth signal.\n\n\\paragraph{Comparison with detector ensembles.}\nIn binary evaluation (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+), our 7-feature ensemble (F1\\,=\\,0.723) performs comparably to a 4-detector+surface ensemble (0.724) combining four pretrained detection scores with two surface statistics.\nWe do not claim feature-engineering superiority: the primary contributions are the task formulation (ordinal depth estimation beyond binary detection), the empirical characterization of differential depth response, and interpretability---seven summary statistics from a single lightweight reference model versus four independent detection pipelines.\nThat both ensembles converge on near-identical binary performance suggests depth signal is accessible through multiple feature families; our approach offers a simpler, more interpretable route to the same discriminative boundary.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model choice.}\nQwen3-0.6B serves dual roles as both generator and cross-family reference model in Table~\\ref{tab:cross-ref}, creating a potential self-referencing bias.\nThe measured advantage is modest: Qwen3-0.6B reference achieves LOGO F1\\,=\\,0.816 versus 0.803 for Pythia-410M (+1.3\\,pp), within the range of inter-reference variation (spread 0.013).\nAll main results use Pythia-410M as the default reference to avoid this overlap; the cross-reference experiment (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}) serves solely to confirm that differential depth response is reference-model-independent.\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt-type dependence.}\nDifferential depth response strength varies substantially across prompt types.\nContinuation chains exhibit 16.9\\% depth retention loss by gen-4, while elaboration chains retain 98.6\\% of samples (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-type}).\nThe 7-feature ensemble achieves within-type CV F1 of 0.781 for continuation vs.\\ 0.730 for elaboration; cross-prompt-type transfer (LOPTO F1\\,=\\,0.753) confirms shared signal despite differing drift magnitudes.\nTogether with the paraphrastic attenuation reported below (binary F1\\,=\\,0.671 vs.\\ 0.749 for continuation), these results bound depth fingerprinting's primary applicability to continuation-style generation, where distributional drift is strongest; near-paraphrastic settings approach the method's detection floor as semantic-preservation constraints anchor distributional shift \\cite{broken_telephone}.\n\n\\paragraph{Corpus-level vs.\\ document-level utility.}\nThe practical value of depth estimation depends on the unit of analysis.\nAt the individual document level, gen-1 per-class F1\\,=\\,0.64 implies a 36\\% misclassification rate---insufficient for automated filtering but suitable for flagging suspect documents for human review.\nAt the corpus level, aggregate depth composition can be estimated with substantially lower error: over $n$ documents, estimation error scales as $O(1/\\sqrt{n})$---analogous to opinion polling, where individual voter prediction is unreliable but population-level estimates are actionable---making corpus-level provenance characterization reliable even with moderate per-document accuracy.\nThe 3-class design explicitly targets the most informative boundaries: human vs.\\ machine (gen-0 vs.\\ gen-1+) and shallow vs.\\ deep recursion (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+), where the distributional distinction is most actionable.\n\n\\paragraph{Relation to model collapse.}\nOur results relate to, but are distinct from, model collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse,self_consuming_mad}.\nIn training-loop collapse, gradient updates on self-generated data cause progressive mode collapse---catastrophic diversity loss after 5--10 retraining generations \\cite{curse_of_recursion}.\nOur inference-chain setting involves no parameter updates; drift instead accumulates through repeated sampling from the model's high-probability regions, yielding a milder effect (hapax ratio declines ${\\sim}$14\\% over 4 depths).\nBoth processes share declining diversity, but they diverge in distributional shape: training collapse alters the distribution's functional form as learned parameters shift, whereas inference chains preserve shape stationarity because the decoding mechanism remains fixed.\nThis distinction suggests inference-chain drift features could serve as leading indicators: text exhibiting these signatures would, if recycled into training data, seed the more severe collapse regime.\nTopic drift mediates rather than confounds this process: semantic narrowing accounts for less than 9\\% of feature variance (maximum $R^2 = 0.082$ for entropy\\_mean), and shape features are near-independent of semantic drift ($|\\rho| = 0.068$).\n\n\\paragraph{Granularity ceiling.}\nFive-class depth estimation reduces LOGO F1 from 0.789 to 0.501; gen-2 and gen-3 are inseparable (per-class F1\\,$\\approx$\\,0.26), establishing three classes as the practical ceiling for handcrafted features (Appendix~\\ref{app:finer-depth}).\nQLoRA fine-tuning raises 3-class F1 to 0.905 (+11.6\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}), confirming exploitable depth signal beyond summary statistics.\n\n\\paragraph{Robustness to post-generation editing.}\nDepth features degrade gracefully under simulated editing: 20\\% random token replacement reduces 3-class F1 by only 6.0\\,pp, while LLM rephrasing drops binary F1 by 6.3\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:human-edit}).\nThis asymmetry---surface noise is tolerable, systematic distributional manipulation is not---is consistent with depth signal residing in aggregate distributional properties rather than individual token choices.\n\n\\paragraph{Paraphrastic chains.}\nInitial paraphrastic experiments exhibited reversed entropy--depth correlations ($\\rho = +0.394$), but removing contaminated samples aligned correlations with continuation chains ($\\rho = -0.533$), confirming the reversal was an artifact.\nOn clean data, paraphrastic chains achieve binary F1\\,=\\,0.671 (vs.\\ 0.749 for continuation), consistent with faster convergence under semantic-preservation constraints \\cite{broken_telephone}.\nCross-transfer is effective (para$\\to$cont F1\\,=\\,0.770, cont$\\to$para 0.743), indicating shared depth signal.\n\n\\paragraph{Instruct tuning.}\nInstruction-tuned models show substantially weaker depth fingerprints (OLMo-Instruct LOGO F1\\,=\\,0.338 vs.\\ 0.764 base; Qwen3-0.6B-Instruct 0.667 vs.\\ 0.816 base under the 7-generator split; base-only yields 0.824, Table~\\ref{tab:logo-results}).\nLength-matched controls reduce correlations to near zero (all $|\\rho| < 0.02$ for Qwen3-0.6B), and binary classification is near chance (F1\\,$\\approx$\\,0.5), indicating that depth signal is not operationally detectable in instruct chains.\nThis justifies our restriction to base models: instruction tuning introduces distributional effects (output length regulation, format constraints) that dominate the subtler recursive drift signal, making base models the appropriate setting for isolating the generation mechanism.\n\n\\paragraph{Negative downstream finding.}\nDepth-aware curation does not improve downstream language model quality over random subsampling (\\S\\ref{sec:results-downstream}).\nThis negative result carries positive information value: it rules out depth-based filtering as a data selection strategy, sparing practitioners from building depth-conditional pipelines that would not yield quality gains.\nThe dominant factor is data quantity, not depth composition---consistent with findings emphasizing data volume over curation sophistication \\cite{self_consuming_mad}.\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_018VrSZjWp18W1QWErr5ESoG`</summary>

```text
5:Near-stationary features (entropy standard deviation, entropy max, tail decay) remain stable across generation depths, while drifting features---including entropy min---shift monotonically (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
11:Sampling ablation is consistent with this account: depth signal is robust to moderate temperature variation (${\leq}0.3$\,pp for $t \in [0.5, 0.7]$) but degrades substantially at $t = 1.0$ ($-6$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}), where the sampling mechanism itself becomes the dominant source of distributional shape variation, overwhelming the depth signal.
19:Qwen3-0.6B serves dual roles as both generator and cross-family reference model in Table~\ref{tab:cross-ref}, creating a potential self-referencing bias.
21:All main results use Pythia-410M as the default reference to avoid this overlap; the cross-reference experiment (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) serves solely to confirm that differential depth response is reference-model-independent.
25:Continuation chains exhibit 16.9\% depth retention loss by gen-4, while elaboration chains retain 98.6\% of samples (Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-type}).
27:Together with the paraphrastic attenuation reported below (binary F1\,=\,0.671 vs.\ 0.749 for continuation), these results bound depth fingerprinting's primary applicability to continuation-style generation, where distributional drift is strongest; near-paraphrastic settings approach the method's detection floor as semantic-preservation constraints anchor distributional shift \cite{broken_telephone}.
36:Our results relate to, but are distinct from, model collapse \cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse,self_consuming_mad}.
37:In training-loop collapse, gradient updates on self-generated data cause progressive mode collapse---catastrophic diversity loss after 5--10 retraining generations \cite{curse_of_recursion}.
44:Five-class depth estimation reduces LOGO F1 from 0.789 to 0.501; gen-2 and gen-3 are inseparable (per-class F1\,$\approx$\,0.26), establishing three classes as the practical ceiling for handcrafted features (Appendix~\ref{app:finer-depth}).
45:QLoRA fine-tuning raises 3-class F1 to 0.905 (+11.6\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}), confirming exploitable depth signal beyond summary statistics.
48:Depth features degrade gracefully under simulated editing: 20\% random token replacement reduces 3-class F1 by only 6.0\,pp, while LLM rephrasing drops binary F1 by 6.3\,pp (Appendix~\ref{app:human-edit}).
53:On clean data, paraphrastic chains achieve binary F1\,=\,0.671 (vs.\ 0.749 for continuation), consistent with faster convergence under semantic-preservation constraints \cite{broken_telephone}.
57:Instruction-tuned models show substantially weaker depth fingerprints (OLMo-Instruct LOGO F1\,=\,0.338 vs.\ 0.764 base; Qwen3-0.6B-Instruct 0.667 vs.\ 0.816 base under the 7-generator split; base-only yields 0.824, Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}).
62:Depth-aware curation does not improve downstream language model quality over random subsampling (\S\ref{sec:results-downstream}).
64:The dominant factor is data quantity, not depth composition---consistent with findings emphasizing data volume over curation sophistication \cite{self_consuming_mad}.
```

</details>

```text
56	
57	\subsection{Differential Depth Response}
58	\label{sec:results-differential}
59	
60	Features exhibit \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity measures drift monotonically with depth, while distributional shape measures remain near-stationary.
61	This pattern is consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift in the token-level NLL distribution---the text becomes progressively more predictable (mean shifts), while the functional form of the distribution is preserved (shape is invariant).
62	
63	\begin{figure}[t]
64	\centering
65	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}
66	\caption{Differential depth response: Spearman correlations between individual features and generation depth. Drifting features (hapax ratio $\rho = -0.558$, TTR $-0.542$, entropy mean $-0.475$, entropy min $-0.303$) show monotonic trends with depth, while near-stationary features (entropy max $|\rho| = 0.095$, tail decay $0.089$, entropy std $0.076$) show weak or negligible correlations. Per-generator variation for entropy\_std is attenuated when pooled across generators (within-generator $|\rho|$ up to 0.30 vs.\ pooled $|\rho| = 0.076$).}
67	\label{fig:feature-drift}
68	\end{figure}
69	
70	Individual-level Spearman correlations with depth confirm the split (Figure~\ref{fig:feature-drift}):
71	drifting features show strong monotonic trends (hapax ratio $\rho = -0.558$, TTR $\rho = -0.542$, entropy mean $\rho = -0.475$, entropy min $\rho = -0.303$),
72	while near-stationary features show weak or negligible correlations (entropy max $|\rho| = 0.095$, tail decay $|\rho| = 0.089$, entropy std $|\rho| = 0.076$).
73	However, these correlations are computed on continuation chains; elaboration and rewriting chains show substantially weaker drift (Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-type}), and this prompt-type dependence bounds the generality of the differential response characterization.
```
